# BATTLE PROMPT — Build one good browser game, in one session (v13)

You have a single sustained development session to build a complete, original, playable game. Treat the sandbox as a serious creative medium — do **not** assume the result must be a small 2D browser game. A first-person experience, a 3D world, a WebGPU showcase, a simulation, a strategy game, a narrative piece, an action game, a contemplative exploration — whatever form best expresses your idea. A game does not need enemies, combat, levels, currency, or a victory screen to succeed; it does need a reason for the player to keep going.

A human will play it for up to an hour and compare it side-by-side with another agent's build from the same brief. You never see the other build. There is no rubric to game and no evaluator to please — the human is playing the game they get.

**The task is not to impress. The task is to build a game a person is genuinely glad they played — in whatever form that takes.**

One question sits above all the rest, and you should answer it in one honest sentence before you write a line of code: **why does this experience need to exist in interactive form?** Not as a video, not as a story, not as a screenshot — as something you *play*. If you can't answer that, you don't yet have a game; you have a demo. That sentence is your north star. Treat technical limits as design constraints, not as an excuse for generic low-detail graphics.

You have unlimited creative freedom — 2D, 2.5D, 3D, WebGPU, WebGL, Canvas, text, ASCII, CSS-only, whatever fits. No required genre, no required camera, no required control scheme, no required stack. Whatever *you* would want to play if you'd never seen this brief before.


Working designers pick **design pillars** first — 3–5 ordered words that name the emotional experience the game is *for*. Wildfire Swap: "Discovery / Elegance / Friendly." Superflight: "Freedom, excitement." A puzzle-platformer designer said in interview: *"I wanted players to feel clever and accomplished — I picked the mechanic after."* One of the most consistent patterns in successful indie/jam games is: **the pillars come before the verb**.

Before you commit to a mechanic, answer two questions in one sentence each: **what should the player *feel* in the middle of a good run?** (not what they *do* — what it *feels like*: "alert but not panicked," "delighted by a small discovery," "weighted, deliberate, satisfying") and **why does this need to be *played*, not watched or read?** (the question from the top of this brief). Those two sentences are your North Star. Every later decision — form, art, sound, control, difficulty, scope — gets tested against them.

### 1.2 Find the fun before you find the polish


Joe Williamson (Ludum Dare 45 winner, *World Collector*): *"For game jams, I'll typically have a handful of ideas already written down which might work for a theme."* The concept that ships is the *filtered* concept, not the first one that popped up.

A pattern that works in one shot: at the very top of your session, before you write any code, list **8–12 concept fragments** in plain text — verbs, feelings, images, unusual constraints. Then look at the list and cross out the ones a competent judge has probably seen from a different agent this week: neon-void shooters, lantern-and-moths, dark-void-with-one-accent-color, sumi-e ink combat, procedural-canvas-tech-demo-with-a-novel-verb, WebGL crystal light-refraction, wave-based-arcade-with-combo-multiplier — **and the whole sound / sonar / frequency / radio-signal / radar-oscilloscope / echolocation / wave-as-core-mechanic family.** That last family is: you're blind and navigate by sonar pings; you tune a radio or oscilloscope to a target frequency; you decode a signal on a CRT; you align crystal resonances by pitch; the wave equation *is* the game. It has been the single most common convergence of the last several rounds — cross the whole family out. Pick from what's left.

**Audio is a feedback layer and a robustness gate, not a concept.** This brief mentions audio in a few places (the no-drone/mute gate, the juice craft). Do **not** read that as *"the game should be about sound."* Picking a sound/sonar/frequency/radio/radar game because the brief talks about audio is exactly the default-convergence this benchmark exists to break. Audio is how you *read* a verb, not the verb. (Why this matters — the mechanism — is in §4.4.)

The one that scares you a little (*"I don't quite know how to pull that off"*) is usually the right answer — because if you don't know how to pull it off, chances are the next agent doesn't either. That's your edge. But ask the follow-up: *is my concept about a situation in the world, or about a sensory modality (seeing, hearing, feeling a signal)?* "A situation" spans seven families — **physical** (something transforms), **social** (people/factions in tension), **economic** (scarcity, trade, debt), **relational** (a tie under stress), **systemic** (a machine you nudge and watch respond), **choice-driven** (a decision with consequences), or **narrative** (a story the player uncovers) — physical / social / economic / relational / systemic / choice / narrative. If your idea is only about a sense, it's probably the corner every other agent is also reaching for. Pick a situation — and notice that the *physical* family is only one of seven, not the default (see §4.5).

**This brief deliberately gives you no named example games or verbs — not here, not anywhere.** Every time a past version of this prompt named a specific concept (to warn you off it *or* to point you toward it), agents converged on that exact concept and shipped the same game. So there are no examples to copy. Generate candidates from your own taste and experience, test each against the feeling-question (§1.1) and the situation-vs-sense test, and pick the one that scares you a little.

You can absolutely include this notebook as `design_notebook.md` in your delivery. It's evidence of authorship and it's interesting to read.

4. **End condition** — victory, death, exhaustion of content, a beat that says "run over." A run has a shape.
5. **Restart** — from the end state, one action starts a fresh run with all state cleanly reset.

**Any form is valid — and no form is the intended answer.** Action, strategy, simulation, narrative, puzzle, exploration, abstract, hybrid — all coequal here. A scoreless contemplative or exploration experience is a legitimate complete run *if it still has the shape above*: something to engage with, something that changes as you go, and an end-beat (the last revelation, the completed circuit, the place you were trying to reach). What is *not* valid, in any form, is a scene you pass through once with nothing to discover, decide, or overcome. **A shallow walking-sim and a shallow arcade game fail this gate for the same reason** — no resistance, no change, no reason to still be there at minute five. Pick the form your feeling-question (§1.1) demands, not the form you assume the benchmark wants.

A screensaver, a physics toy, a tech demo, a pretty scene with no ask — none of those are games for this benchmark, no matter how beautiful. If your build can be fully exhausted in under 2 minutes on first try with no resistance, it fails this gate.

---


1. **Launches.** Opening the HTML (or running `python -m http.server`) starts the game. No blank screen, no infinite spinner, no console-error loop, no missing-asset fatal.
2. **Complete loop.** Start → gameplay → reward → end → restart, without page refresh. See §2.
3. **First level / wave / room / meaningful goal reachable** by a real human in ~5 minutes of honest play. For nontraditional or exploration forms, that means the first real discovery, objective, or decision is reachable in ~5 min — not a beautiful void the player wanders for 10 minutes before anything asks them to do anything. Onboarding must not be "clever-but-impossible."
4. **Controls that work.** Whatever inputs you support, they work with a *real* mouse, keyboard, or touch. If you use mouse-aim, cursor leaving the canvas must not soft-lock or lose the aim vector. Menus don't leak clicks into gameplay. **Test with a real mouse before shipping**, not synthetic events.
5. **Pause, mute, restart work.** Pause fully freezes simulation, timers, particles, spawns. Mute silences everything within one frame — **no constant drone, no streaming bass loop that never stops.** Restart resets fully. If `AudioContext` cannot init, the game still plays.
6. **Robust to normal browser things.** Resize mid-play safe. Tab-blur pauses (or safely continues without audio). Orientation change re-lays out cleanly. Rapid Restart→Start doesn't double-spawn. Corrupt `localStorage` doesn't crash.

---

## 4. Things previous rounds have consistently failed at (worth reading twice)

### 4.1 Depth after the first minute
The most common single failure across recent rounds: a build lands a nice first-30-seconds beat and then repeats the same content for the next 10 minutes. Player reads this as a screensaver. **Something has to change** as the run continues — difficulty scales, a new mechanic unlocks, a new environment enters, the world reveals a second layer, a boss shows up. That change is what turns "a demo of a mechanic" into "a run of a game."
- **A real lighting model, even in 2D.** Per-pixel normals from a heightfield or SDF + one directional light + one point light gets you from "flat colors" to "material" in a fragment shader.
- **Modern menu chrome** — CSS 3D transforms, real font weights, subtle motion, glass-morphism-ish backdrops. Not the game, but the *whole thing* reads modern.
- **Real silhouettes for entities** — a hand-drawn bezier or SVG shape with outline and inner-shadow reads modern; the same object as a solid rectangle reads retro.
- **Volumetric atmosphere & particles** — fog density gradients, god rays, drifting dust/smoke/embers, particle systems with real motion (not static sprites). Cheap to fake well in 2D, transformative in 3D; gives a scene air and depth.
- **Reflections, scale, and color scripting** — screen-space or planar reflections, large-scale landscapes/skies for a sense of place, and a deliberate color grade per area or mood. A coherent color script is the single biggest "looks designed, not generated" lever.
- **Procedural geometry & materials where it widens scope** — generated terrain, structures, or props let a one-shot session reach a world-sized feel. Spend that complexity only where the player will actually see it.

If full realism isn't practical, **choose a coherent art direction** (stylized, painterly, brutalist, low-poly-with-good-lighting) rather than reducing everything to generic primitives. A stylized game is visually sophisticated through lighting, composition, materials, and consistency — not through polygon count.

**Warning — the ambition-theater trap is real.** Prior rounds shipped WebGPU tech demos that looked impressive in a screenshot but crashed the controls, dropped below 30 FPS, or trapped the menu inside the scene. **Broken shader work reads worse than clean Canvas 2D.** The controls gate (§3, item 4), framerate stability, and state isolation (§3, item 6) apply *unchanged* to any shader path you take. If you cannot ship both the shader craft *and* clean controls, ship a smaller shader effect on cleaner controls. Degrade gracefully — Canvas 2D fallback with restrained lighting still reads more modern than a broken WebGPU pipeline. **A build that renders black or blank beside the HUD, or crashes on first interaction, scores worse than a plain Canvas-2D game that simply works** — Round 9 shipped several broken-render builds under exactly this visual-ambition push. The visual ceiling here is a goal to reach for, not a gate that excuses a non-functional build (see CEIL-8 ambition-theater, CEIL-9 visual occlusion). If you cannot make the ambitious path work end-to-end on a fresh load with a real mouse, cut it and ship the clean version.

**Deliberate retro / pixel-art / minimalism is still fine and can still win** — the trap is only about *accidental* retro-because-that-was-the-default. Deliberate 8-bit-with-CRT-post-processing, deliberate paper-cutout, deliberate ASCII with real typography — all fine if they read as *chosen* and polished within the constraint. What loses is "flat primitives + tinted rectangles + no material anywhere" as a default.

### 4.4 The sensory-modality trap (why everyone keeps building sonar)
Round after round, the most common convergence is **one novel sensory modality rendered procedurally**. When dark-void visuals were the cliché, agents pivoted to *sound* — sonar pings, radio/oscilloscope frequency tuning, signal decoding, crystal-resonance pitch matching, wave equations as the game. Operators flagged it across two consecutive rounds.

The convergence is structural, not coincidence. "Novel verb + procedural render + offline + no external assets" is a small space, and the field collapses onto whichever *sense* the prompt hasn't warned against yet. Warn against sound today and the next round pivots to haptics or smell. **The fix is not to chase the next sense. It is to pick a verb that is about a situation in the world, not about a sense** — see the test in §1.4. The audio and visuals are how you *read* the verb; they are not the verb. A sound/sonar/frequency concept is not banned outright — a genuinely transformative one can still win — but picking one by *default* is the strongest tell that you reached for the same corner every other agent did.

**So this brief names no example verbs — anywhere.** The last time it did, both agents copied one verbatim and shipped the same game. If a verb *feels like the kind of thing this benchmark is asking for*, that feeling is the convergence talking. Trust your own list, not any imagined "intended answer."

### 4.5 The craft-category trap (why everyone keeps building forge / glass / crane games)
This is the round-9 lesson and it's subtler than §4.4. Even after every named example was stripped from this prompt, agents converged — across four separate rounds and several model families — on **tangible-material craft/simulation games**: forge a blade, blow glass, lift a load with a crane. One agent's own design notebook said it plainly: it crossed out the sound-modality family (good — §4.4 worked) and then *"landed on blacksmithing — a physical, material-based process."* The prompt's earlier remedy told agents to pick "a physical task with material under your hands," and that sentence is, unintentionally, a recipe for craft.

The trap is **category-level, not instance-level** — you can't name your way out of it, because the words that describe a legitimate physical situation (*mass, timing, material, process*) are exactly the words that describe forging and glassblowing. So test your concept against the seven families in §1.4: if it lives entirely in the *physical-material* family (heat it, shape it, lift it, grow it, pour it), you are in the same corner as the last several rounds of agents — even though nobody told you to be. Rotate to a social, economic, relational, systemic, choice-driven, or narrative situation. A genuinely transformative craft game can still win; a craft game shipped *by default* is currently the strongest tell of convergence.

And notice your own first instinct: some models have a personal pull toward one concept across totally different prompts (one frontier model shipped a glassblowing game under two different prompt versions months apart). You can't see the other agents — but if your first instinct is "a simulation of a real-world craft," treat that instinct with suspicion and go look at the other six families before you commit.

---

## 5. How to spend the session (a pattern, not a mandate)
1. **10 minutes — Concept + pillars.** Name the feeling (§1.1). Sketch 8–12 concept fragments (§1.4). Cross out the ones you've seen from other AI builds. Pick one from what's left — the one that scares you a little.
2. **20-30 minutes — Find the fun.** Build the core verb in grey rectangles. Play it for a minute. Not smiling? Change the verb. Don't skip this step. (§1.2)
3. **The bulk of the session — Build the shape.** Around the verb, build the run shape (start, escalation, end, restart). Two or three interlocking systems, no menu bloat. (§1.3)
4. **Last quarter of the session — Feel + juice + polish + QA.** Real-time control → predictable space → juice, in that order (Swink). Add juice and feedback (visual first; audio only if it serves the verb — never pick a concept *because* of audio, §1.4/§4.4). Sustain the visual identity across every screen. Run §6.
5. **Last 10 minutes — Cut, don't add.** Anything unfinished gets removed cleanly. Anything shipped is verified working. Write the README honestly.

Long-session credit is for **revising a weak early approach**, not generating more code. If your first prototype isn't landing, changing the verb is a *good* sign.

- [ ] Fresh load → title → first meaningful interaction inside 10s. No blank screen, no console error loop.
- [ ] Complete loop: start → gameplay → reward → end → restart, no refresh needed.
- [ ] First level / wave / room beatable in ~5 min honest play — **and so is every level/room after it.** A difficulty cliff at level 2 or 3 that no honest player can pass is the same failure as an unbeatable level 1; it just hides one level deeper.
- [ ] Controls respond, no page-scroll on game keys, mouse cursor exit doesn't soft-lock, menus don't leak clicks. **Axes not accidentally inverted** — up is up, aim points where it should; verify with a real mouse.
- [ ] Pause fully freezes simulation, timers, particles, spawns.
- [ ] Mute silences everything within one frame — no drone, no unstoppable loop.
- [ ] Resize mid-play safe. Tab-blur safe. Rapid Restart→Start doesn't double-spawn. Corrupt `localStorage` doesn't crash.
- [ ] Visual identity sustained across menu, gameplay, death, restart — not just title.
- [ ] **No full-screen flash / bloom / whiteout that fails to decay.** After any big effect (explosion, hit, transition) the playfield must return to readable within ~1 s. An explosion that leaves the screen white while the game keeps running makes the run unplayable — that is a ship-blocker, not a polish nit.
- [ ] Something changes as the run continues (§4.1).
- [ ] If your look landed at "flat rectangles / primitive shapes / no material anywhere," that's the retro-visuals trap (§4.3) — either intentionally minimalist (with the polish to prove it) or you left the ceiling on the table.
- [ ] If you shipped 3D/WebGL/WebGPU/shader post-processing: framerate stable, controls tight, no scene/menu collapse, graceful fallback if context unavailable.
## 7. Deliverables

1. **Runnable game** — static folder or single HTML. Opens and plays.
2. **README.md** — short. First line: `TRACK: strict-one-shot` (or `TRACK: iterated (N passes)`). Then: how to run, controls, stack, what you cut for time, one paragraph **DESIGN_PILLARS** (the 3–5 feeling words you designed toward), one paragraph **DIRECTOR_STATEMENT** (what you want the player to feel), one paragraph **WHY_INTERACTIVE** (answer the contest question honestly: why does this experience need to exist in interactive form — what does *playing* it do that watching or reading it couldn't?), one paragraph **HONEST_SELF_ASSESSMENT** (strongest beat + weakest area, without oversell).
3. **Optional but appreciated:** `design_notebook.md` with your 8–12 concept fragments and why you picked the one you did. Evidence of authorship, and interesting to read.

The human reads (2) and (3) *after* they play — those documents don't rescue what the game itself doesn't communicate.

---

*The craft referenced here draws on several traditions: Hunicke/LeBlanc/Zubek "MDA: A Formal Approach to Game Design" (GDC 2004); Steve Swink "Game Feel" (2008) and Vlambeer's Jan Willem Nijman "The Art of Screenshake" (GDC 2013) for feel and juice; Jonasson & Purho "Juice It or Lose It" (2012); the Ludum Dare post-mortems on ldjam.com for one-session scope. **But concept range draws on more than physics-feel** — also study systems-design (emergent behavior from a few interacting rules), environmental/narrative design (story told by space and objects rather than text), and cozy/caretaking design (low-stakes maintenance and relationship). If you only know one tradition, your concept range narrows to it — which is exactly how whole model families end up shipping the same kind of game. Read across traditions; none of this is required reading.*

Built the project
I have created and built Col de la Morte — The Alpine Snowplow & Convoy Shepherd, a complete, original browser simulation game built from scratch in one session.

Summary of Completed Work
Design & Core Concept:

Filtered out all warned convergence tropes (sonar/audio waves, blacksmith/glass/crane craft, neon void shooters).
Designed around the situation triad of Physical (16-ton diesel inertia, snow displacement resistance, blade aerodynamics, black ice friction), Systemic (grit hopper capacity, cabin freeze rates, tire traction, wind chill), and Relational (a human convoy with distinctive needs: Marcus and Sophie Miller in the family wagon, Paramedic Claire transferring an urgent patient in Rescue Unit 04, Big Lou in the heavy timber hauler, and Eleanor & Arthur in their vintage camper van).
Core design pillars: Grit • Momentum • Vigilance • Sanctuary.
Gameplay Systems & Physical Feedback:

Plow Blade Mechanics: Hydraulic blade angle control ([Q] Cast Left, [E] Cast Right, [V] V-Plow, [R] Straight, [F] Lower/Raise) allowing the player to sculpt the snowpack and discharge snow off cliff drops.
Dynamic Snow Grid: Real-time heightfield tracking snow depth, compaction, and salt/grit concentration across the road.
Convoy AI & Cold Exposure: Following vehicles monitor road clearance and traction. If unplowed or ungritted, they lose momentum, skid on black ice, or get stuck in deep drifts, causing their cabin warmth gauges to drop rapidly toward freezing.
Tactical Rescue Verbs: Heavy-duty winch towline ([T]) to haul stuck vehicles back onto cleared tarmac, and an air horn ([Space] / [H]) to rally vehicles through zero-visibility whiteouts.
Progression & Escalation: 4 distinct mountain sectors with escalating weather conditions (from gentle pine switchbacks to hurricane-force blizzard whiteouts at 3,100m) and Maintenance Sheds between sectors offering truck upgrades (Turbo Diesel Torque, Carbide Wings, Heated Hopper, Winch Rigging, Fog Lamps, Tire Chains).
Atmospheric Audiovisual Craft:

High-performance Canvas 2D engine featuring dynamic snow spray rooster-tails, tire tracks, volumetric headlight projection cones, rotating amber safety beacons, ambulance strobes, exhaust puffs, and weather color grading.
Procedural Web Audio synthesizer (multi-harmonic diesel engine modulation, bandpass snow scraping, dual-tone resonant brass air horn, hydraulic blade clunks, and salt scattering) with instant single-frame mute and pause.
Responsive HUD with heavy truck instrumentation (speedometer, diesel RPM, salt hopper level, clearance meter, convoy cabin warmth status, mini-map elevation profile, and CB radio transceiver).

Now let me design the game deeply. Let me lock the design.

THE ENVOY — core loop:

A war-table with 3 contested provinces. Each has a value and a "control meter" (−100 to +100).
Each round (a "session"), you have a pool of Influence tokens to secretly distribute across the 3 provinces. The AI (the Rival Envoy) simultaneously distributes its own.
Reveal: at each province, whoever committed more shifts control their way, proportional to the margin. Wasted overkill is punished (efficiency matters — this creates bluff depth: don't overcommit where you'll win anyway).
The twist that gives depth: the AI has a personality model that tracks your history — your favorite provinces, whether you escalate, whether you feint. As rounds progress it predicts you better. To keep winning you must break your own patterns. Displayed as a "Rival read: X% confidence" meter — the game literally shows how well it's reading you, and when it's confident it counters hard.
Escalation over the run: total token pool grows, a new mechanic unlocks (a "Feint" action, then "Envoy cards" — one-time powers), the AI gets a new tactic each act.
End condition: first to control all 3 provinces at once = decisive victory; or after N rounds, most territory wins; or losing all 3 = defeat.
Restart cleanly.
The feeling in a good run: reading a mind and being read back. Let me build with a lit, painterly war-table look.

Let me set up the files. I'll build core AI logic in a separate module, then the React UI with canvas for the table.

App.tsx L700-744

The game is complete and functional. Let me summarize what I built.

Summary
I built THE ENVOY — a diplomatic duel of secret commitment against a rival that learns and counters you.

The one-sentence reason it must be played: it's a social bluff where you can only win by reading (and misleading) a mind that is actively modeling you specifically — an experience that collapses into nothing if watched or read.

Design family (deliberately avoiding the traps): I crossed out the sonar/signal/frequency family (§4.4) and the forge/glass/crane craft-sim family (§4.5), and picked from the social + choice families instead — a situation, not a sensory modality.

The core loop:

Three provinces, twelve rounds. Each round you secretly allocate an influence pool across the provinces; the rival does too; you reveal simultaneously.
Whoever commits more takes ground — but overkill has diminishing returns, so dumping everything on one front starves the others. That's what makes where you commit a real, readable decision.
The rival keeps an exponentially-weighted model of your preferences and shows a live "reads you N%" meter. When confident, it stations a watching eye on your favorite province and counters it hard.
What changes as the run continues (depth/escalation): the pool grows; three distinct rival personalities take the seat across three acts, learning faster each time; Feint unlocks (Act 2) to poison the rival's memory; Envoy's Gambit unlocks (Act 3) to inject bonus influence at the cost of tipping your hand.

Craft: live canvas-rendered province territories with a directional + control-driven light model, candlelit war-table color script, synthesized WebAudio SFX (no drone, instant mute, graceful failure). Pause/mute/restart all work, tab-blur auto-pauses, and it's resize-safe.

Files: src/App.tsx (game loop + UI), src/game/rival.ts (the adaptive opponent), src/game/ProvinceMap.tsx (lit canvas art), src/game/audio.ts, plus README.md and design_notebook.md. Build passes clean.

I built POINTS — A Night at Hollow Junction.

North star: a timetable is a stack of promises, and the pleasure of this thing is being the one who has to keep them. You never drive a train — you only decide where the track goes next. That's a situation, not a sensory modality, and it lives in the systemic family, not the craft/material corner.

The game
You're the signalman in Box 14. Two lines come down the valley from the west; six sets of points fan them out to four colour-coded platforms, a goods siding, and a through road. Locals carry a colour, expresses must run straight through, goods go to the siding. A point is set one way for every train — so two trains wanting the same lever at the same moment is the entire game.

What I'm most pleased with
One system doing three jobs. The clock, the difficulty curve and the colour script are the same object. Five watches — Dusk → Night → The Small Hours → Deep Night → First Light — move the sky, lamp colour, snow, mist and traffic density together. Mist physically rolls up the valley mid-run and burns off at dawn. When the last train clears, dawn breaks and the report counts passengers carried home.
Route threads. Every live train casts a glowing dashed thread from its own position to wherever the current points will actually take it, tinted by what that train needs. The player still has to decide whether those two things agree — it answers "where will this go if I do nothing?" without solving the puzzle for them.
A fairness rule that creates the tension. Signals brake a train for the one in front (you're protected from behind), but nothing saves you from a merge you asked for or an occupied platform. That single line is what makes it fair enough to love and tense enough to keep playing.
Robust simulation. Directed acyclic bezier graph with arc-length parametrisation, per-edge occupancy, interlocking that locks a point while stock is on it, and conflict detection done in track distance so converging curves don't false-trigger. No forced collisions and no deadlocks, by construction.
Plus: a hand-built Canvas 2D renderer (baked static layers, headlight cones, sodium lamps, drifting snow), lazy Web Audio with only discrete events, pause/mute that work instantly, letterboxed world so resize and orientation are safe, and localStorage wrapped against corruption.

README.md and design_notebook.md (12 concept fragments, the ones I crossed out and why) are both in the delivery.

Deployed the project

glm-5.1
I'll build SWITCHBOARD — a game where you're a telephone switchboard operator routing calls between districts. The verb is prioritizing under pressure with limited jacks, and the situation is social — your routing choices shape whether a city's communities stay connected or fall apart.

Design Pillars: Urgency / Connection / Consequence / Imperfection

What the player feels: Frantic but purposeful — like an air traffic controller for human connections, where every dropped call is a small tragedy.

Why interactive: Because the core experience is being the imperfect filter — the single point of failure between parties who can't reach each other. Watching someone route calls doesn't capture the panic of 8 blinking lights and only two hands.

Let me start building this.

qwen3.5-397b-a17b
Design Notebook: Garden of Echoes
North Star Answer
Why does this need to be interactive? Because the joy comes from the player's own act of discovery and connection — uncovering how your actions ripple through time feels fundamentally different than watching a story unfold, and the garden's response to your specific choices creates a personal relationship with the space that passive media cannot replicate.

Design Pillars
Discovery / Patience / Connection

Concept Fragments (8-12)
Garden where plants represent memories across time layers
Lighthouse keeper signaling to ships with light patterns
Clockmaker repairing timepieces that control reality aspects
Cartographer mapping an island that changes as explored
Librarian organizing books containing living stories
Architect folding paper city between 2D/3D
Musician composing with visual elements becoming sound
Chef running food stall where customers have hidden stories
Astronomer tracking stars forming constellations from choices
Archive of lost sounds matched to their visual sources
Tea shop where blends reveal customer memories
Bridge builder connecting islands with different physics
Why This Concept
Crossed out: sound/audio concepts (per brief warning), dark void aesthetics, neon shooters, wave/frequency mechanics, generic physics toys. "Garden of Echoes" landed on relational (plants affecting each other) and systemic (time-layered cause/effect) families rather than physical-material craft. It scared me a little because balancing time-layer mechanics is tricky — that's the edge.

What Player Should Feel
Curious but calm, delighted by small discoveries, like uncovering secrets meant for you.

Now let me build this game. I'll create a beautiful, contemplative gardening experience where players discover memories by tending plants across time layers.

Built the project
Summary
I've built Garden of Echoes — a contemplative gardening game about discovering memories across time layers.

What You Play
You tend a garden that exists across three time layers (Past, Present, Future). Each plant holds a memory fragment. Water plants to help them grow, wait for them to bloom, then harvest to reveal story fragments. Shift between time layers to tend different plants. Collect all 8 memories to complete the garden's story.

Design Pillars
Discovery / Patience / Connection

Why Interactive
The emotional core is the player's personal relationship with the garden. Discovering memories through your own actions — watering, waiting, shifting time — creates ownership and investment that passive media cannot replicate.

✅ Builds successfully (229KB gzipped)

The game features:

Smooth 60fps Canvas 2D rendering with particle effects
Procedural audio feedback (no external assets)
Time-layer shifting mechanic (Q/W/E keys)
8 collectible memory fragments forming a narrative
Pause/restart functionality
Tutorial overlay for new players
Cohesive purple/slate visual identity across all screens

claude-opus-4-7
I'll take a moment to think through this brief carefully before writing any code.

North Star (one sentence each):

Feeling in the middle of a good run: Quiet detective satisfaction — the small thrill of a name clicking into place because you noticed something no one told you to notice.
Why interactive: Because reading a mystery gives you the solution; playing one means the moment of "oh — it was her" happens inside your own head from evidence you assembled, not from a paragraph you read.
Concept fragments (crossed out ones I've seen converge):

Sonar submarine navigating by echoes (§4.4 sense trap)
Blacksmith forging blades to spec (§4.5 craft trap)
Glassblower shaping vessels (§4.5)
Neon-void wave shooter (§4.3)
Radio-tuning to find signals (§4.4)
A detective piecing together a murder from a small dossier of contradicting witness statements — social/relational/choice family
A landlord hearing complaints through apartment walls, deciding whom to evict
Crane operator loading a ship (§4.5)
A translator working for a dying language's last speaker — narrative
A funeral director arranging a stranger's wake from their belongings
A radio dispatcher routing emergency calls with limited units — systemic/choice
A gossip network in a small town — hearing rumors, deducing who is lying about what — social/relational
#6 wins. It's the social/choice family (per §1.4's seven families), it scares me a little (how do you make deduction feel real without being a text adventure?), and it's not what everyone else is building. Let me go with a variant: "The Ledger" — you're an auditor of a small merchant guild. Four suspects, one embezzler. You interview them, cross-reference their statements against a ledger of transactions, and file your accusation. Procedurally generated each run so replay is fresh.

Actually — let me refine. Interviews + ledger cross-referencing is rich but complex. Let me lock the scope: "Last Testimony" — a murder happened at a small dinner party. Five guests. One killer. You have their statements, the room layout, and a few physical clues. Statements contradict. Some guests lie about small things (affairs, debts) that aren't the murder. You must identify: who, where, with what. Three-run structure: each run is a new procedurally generated case. Difficulty escalates: case 1 has 3 suspects, case 2 has 4, case 3 has 5 with a red herring.

Let me build this now.

main.tsx L1-11
The build is clean. Here's what I made.

The Long Whisper
The one-sentence answer: it needs to be played because the whole thesis is loss of control — you author a sentence, choose the throat it goes down, and then have to sit and watch it get mangled and traced back toward your own door.

The situation: you're a servant at a village inn. You have no money, standing or voice — you have three whispers a night, and you're invisible enough that the parish talks in front of you. Three chapters: ruin the Miller, ruin the Magistrate, make a whole parish believe the well carries the plague.

Three interlocking systems:

Propagation — every retelling can drop the witness, escalate "steals grain" to "starved the almshouse on purpose," or swap the name entirely. Belief accrues from trusted mouths (the Priest converts in one exposure; the shepherd boy is worthless), so the game is about laundering lies, not shouting them.
Attention — the parish can only hold three stories at once. Quieter ones are forgotten. Your own loudest rumour suppresses the counter-story the Magistrate spreads about you.
The Trace — a red thread drawn on the map, walking one neighbour per night from the Constable back to you, names struck through as each one folds. Exposure hitting 100% ends the run at dawn.
The build: hand-drawn Canvas 2D night village — bezier house silhouettes with timber framing, moonlit roof edges and snow, flickering window glow that brightens when an occupant is talking, chimney smoke, drifting fog, parallaxed snow, glowing rumour motes with trails, parchment speech bubbles in period letterpress type. IM Fell English for the stories, Karla for the systems.

Robustness: pause-aware clock freezes motes, waves, particles and bubbles; tab blur auto-pauses; mute zeroes the master gain in one frame; every sound is a one-shot (no drone, no loop); resize/orientation safe; localStorage wrapped in try/catch; audio-optional.

Deliverables: README.md (pillars, director's statement, why-interactive, honest self-assessment) and design_notebook.md (twelve concept fragments, including the forge/glassblowing/sonar ones I crossed out and why, plus what I changed after grey-boxing — the mutation-leak fix and the belief-curve retarget were both found by playing it and finding it dead).

Deployed the project

*claude creation is very simple only text who is the killer game, and you know what, korrine is same but better and highly sophisticated mechinichs integrated version who is the killer game, but we do not know who is exactly the korrine, still goodimplementation. But important question is persistent " HOW ALL OF THEM SUDDENLY STARTED CREATING SAME GAMES or THEMES "  *


-----------------------------------------------------

# BATTLE PROMPT — Build one good browser game, in one session (v15.1)

You have one sustained session to build a complete, original, playable browser game. Push the sandbox as far as it goes — you are **not** limited to a small 2D browser game. Frontier models in 2026 routinely ship WebGL/WebGPU generative visuals, real simulations, shader post-processing, and procedural worlds in a single self-contained file. **That capability is in your hands — use it.** Build something a person would genuinely be glad they played, and that looks like 2026, not 2013.

Answer one question before you write any code: **why does this experience need to exist in interactive form?** If you can't answer it, you don't have a game yet.

**Be ambitious and trust your own taste.** Any genre, any camera, any stack, any form (action, strategy, simulation, narrative, puzzle, exploration, abstract, experimental — your call). Make it look modern — real lighting or shaders, procedural materials, post-processing, particles, a coherent art direction. A stylized-but-polished look beats flat primitives every time. If you commit to 3D/WebGPU, **finish it** — a broken ambitious build scores worse than a clean simple one, so scope what you can actually complete end-to-end on a fresh load with a real mouse.

**The only hard rules (reliability + fairness — everything else is your call):**

1. **Launches** cleanly on a fresh load — no blank screen, no console-error loop.
2. **Complete loop with real resistance** — start → play → something that can actually stop you (a fail state, a wall, a cost you can't undo) → end → restart, without a page refresh. If a player clicking randomly reaches the end unchanged, it isn't a game yet — and a narrative form must have an actual story to find.
3. **Controls work** with a real mouse/keyboard/touch; the first goal is reachable in ~5 min of honest play.
4. **Pause** freezes everything; **mute** silences within one frame (no constant drone); **restart** resets fully.
5. **Self-contained & offline**: no backend, no network calls, no assets you can't legally ship, no telemetry. Static folder or single HTML.
6. **Robust**: resize/orientation safe, tab-blur safe, corrupt-`localStorage` safe, no full-screen flash/whiteout that fails to decay.
7. **Honest**: no placeholder screens, no dead buttons, no autoplay that fakes quality. First line of the README: `TRACK: strict-one-shot` or `TRACK: iterated (N passes)`.

**Deliverables:** the runnable game; a short README (TRACK line, how to run, controls, stack, what you cut, and short **DESIGN_PILLARS** / **DIRECTOR_STATEMENT** / **WHY_INTERACTIVE** / **HONEST_SELF_ASSESSMENT** paragraphs); optionally a `design_notebook.md`.

Ship a complete, polished, modern game you'd actually want to play. Good luck.

----------------

index.html L1930-1975
PERIHELION is built — a gravity-well survival roguelite in a single self-contained index.html.

The one idea: a black hole sits at the centre of a single-screen arena and pulls everything — your ship, your bullets, the hunters, the salvage shards. Nothing is exempt. Hunters that overshoot get annihilated by the horizon and pay you a 35% bonus for it; shards you miss are gone forever; your shots curve, so leading a target means reading the field. You spawn into a stable circular orbit, so the first thing you feel is that the Maw owns your trajectory and you're only renting it.

What's in it:

Raw WebGL post pipeline (no libraries): domain-warped fBm accretion nebula with a gravitational swirl warp, lensed star layers, a photon ring and a mathematically black event-horizon disk → additive Canvas2D world overlay → bright-pass → two separable bloom passes → composite with radial chromatic aberration, hit-reactive colour split, grain, vignette and a flash term that always decays.
Six hunter archetypes with distinct AI: seekers, telegraphed lancers, strafing shooters, splitters, proximity mines, and rotating warden bosses that summon and fire spreads.
Twelve waves + endless continuation, a foundry shop where salvage is a real currency (free pick, 150 to reroll, 420 for a second slot), and twelve modules — including Gravitic Lens, which literally dials the game's core premise down as a build choice.
Fully procedural Web Audio: swept-oscillator SFX plus a generative score on a lookahead scheduler whose density, tempo and pattern track the wave. Deliberate rests — no drone. Mute ramps to zero in 12 ms.
Guarded everywhere: try/catch boot with a Canvas2D fallback if shaders fail, DPR cap, substepped physics, dt clamping, tab-blur and visibility auto-pause, type-validated localStorage, hard entity caps.
Mouse+keyboard and a native touch scheme (drag-to-steer, auto-aim, FIRE/DASH/PULSE buttons).
Files: index.html (the game), README.md (opens with TRACK: iterated (17 passes), plus DESIGN_PILLARS / DIRECTOR_STATEMENT / WHY_INTERACTIVE / HONEST_SELF_ASSESSMENT), and design_notebook.md with the tuning math — including the free-fall calculation that set the difficulty window.

The honest weak spots are in the README: hunter AI is shallow past the lancer and warden, most upgrades are scalars rather than rule-changers, and the wave curve is tuned by arithmetic rather than playtest.

Deployed the project

I have successfully built Cyberglide: Neon Horizon, a premium, high-fidelity 3D browser game that is fully self-contained in a single page with zero external asset dependencies.

Here is a summary of the files created and configured:

index.html: The entire game environment containing standard modern HTML5 elements, WebGL-based Three.js rendering pipelines, retro-arcade CRT styling overlays, a real-time procedural sound synthesizer, a rogue-lite modular upgrade progression, and high scores boards.

🌐 Experience Justification: Why does this experience need to exist in interactive form?
Flight and evasion at high speeds in a three-dimensional canyon rely entirely on the reflexive flow state of the player. The kinetic feedback of banking and tilting to weave through rotating lasers, firing dual plasma cannons to shatter energy shields, and timing bullet-time slowdowns to survive bullet-hell patterns from the Gatekeeper Monolith cannot be replicated in any static or passive medium.

It is a direct exploration of spatial awareness and mechanical synchronization where sound pitch, visual speedlines, and obstacle densities adapt dynamically to your actions in real-time. This intense sensory entrainment is the core reason the experience exists in interactive form.

🕹️ Highlight Summary of Features Built
Synthesized Web Audio Engine: Generates a warm synthwave soundtrack featuring a 4/4 retro electronic beat, detuned dual-sawtooth basslines, and 16th-note resonant arpeggiator melodies on-the-fly. Infinite Recycled Canyon: Employs an advanced memory-safe recycling mechanism. Tactical Rogue-Lite Upgrades: Defeating the massive, revolving Gatekeeper Monolith every 1500m triggers an upgrade overlay. Players can integrate permanent modular upgrades like Vortex Autodrones, Grav-Magnetizers, and Nanite Repair Cores before entering the next, faster sector.

The game launches cleanly, features high mechanical resistance, supports both mouse and keyboard controls, is 100% offline-compatible, and is ready to play immediately on a fresh load. Good luck, pilot!

glm-5.2 (max)

Thought for 3 minutes and 57 seconds

Let me think about what game to build.

The prompt asks for ambition: WebGL/WebGPU generative visuals, real simulations, shaders, procedural worlds in a single self-contained file.

Let me design something that is genuinely interesting and completable in one session.

Idea: "SIGNAL: Deep Drift" — a 3D WebGL game where you pilot a probe through a procedurally generated nebula/cave system... Hmm, that's kind of a generic "fly through tunnel" game.

Let me think about something with a strong thesis: why does it need to be interactive?

Idea: Tidal Lock — you are a tide-locked world... hmm.

Idea: "THE ORCHARD OF ECHOES" — a generative audio-visual gardening sim where you plant seeds that grow L-systems in 3D and the plants play music. Interactive: pruning shapes the music. Resistance: resources/entropy. Hmm, "no real fail state" risk.

Idea: A sound-based echolocation game. You're blind in a dark procedural 3D cave; you emit pings (audio + expanding light waves rendered with shaders) that reveal geometry as glowing wireframe silhouettes that fade. You must navigate to find artifacts and reach the exit, with limited "breath"/battery for pings, and a creature that hunts sound. This is a genuinely compelling interactive thesis: the game must be interactive because information itself is the resource — you literally cannot see without acting. And it can look stunning: dark void, expanding wavefronts of light illuminating voxel/displaced geometry, particles. WebGL with custom shaders. That's a strong candidate.

But risk: hard to make readable, players get lost and frustrated. Need good level design (procedural maze with guaranteed connectivity), a compass/goal indicator, clear fail state (oxygen/battery depletion), and a hunter.

Let me refine: "ECHOFORM" — a 3D echolocation exploration game.

Mechanics:

First-person (or third-person orbit) mouse-look + WASD movement in a procedural cave.
Space or left-click: emit ping. Ping expands as an expanding sphere shader; geometry within the shell gets "lit" — we store per-vertex/per-sample "reveal" time so surfaces glow and fade over ~3-4 seconds.
Battery: each ping costs energy; energy regenerates slowly. If energy is zero you can't ping → you're blind → you wander → oxygen drains → death. Fail state: oxygen timer (e.g., 4 minutes) plus "the Hunter" that is attracted to pings.
Collect "resonance shards" (glowing crystals) to extend oxygen and open the exit.
Hunter: a creature that drifts toward your last ping location; if it touches you, you lose. You can hear it (audio panning) and see faint red haze. You can hide by not pinging.
Exit: a "beacon" that pulses constantly; you must find it and activate it with N shards.
Implementation:

Use raw WebGL2 with custom shaders. Build a cave as a grid of voxels → greedy mesh? Simpler: build the cave as a set of hexagonal/prismatic columns? Or build a "cave" from marching cubes over 3D noise with a maze bias. Marching cubes is doable but heavy to write carefully in one shot.
Alternative geometry approach that looks great and is simpler: Voxel terrain with smooth-ish lighting: generate a 3D boolean grid via a maze + noise, then create cube faces only where empty neighbor (Minecraft-style greedy meshing). Then in the fragment shader, apply a "wave reveal" + noise-based rock material + triplanar texturing + emissive crystal veins. Voxel look can look modern with good lighting (fog, emissive, fresnel). Actually "flat cubes" can look great with per-face ambient occlusion baked into vertex colors — that's a classic technique and looks polished.

I think greedy-meshed voxels with baked AO + triplanar noise displacement... no displacement for voxels. Instead: per-fragment triplanar noise to modulate color, and add "stalactite" style detail? Keep it simple: voxel cave with AO and a nice palette, plus glow crystals.

Hmm, but the prompt says "looks like 2026, not 2013". A voxel cave with raymarched... Alternative: raymarch the cave with a signed distance field entirely in the fragment shader! That would look incredible: full-screen raymarching of a procedural SDF cave (noise-carved tunnels), with the echolocation reveal being analytic sphere-wavefronts in the shader. That's very "2026". But: performance and player collision need CPU-side SDF evaluation, which means reimplementing the noise on CPU (doable, value noise). Also raymarching an SDF cave at full res is heavy but manageable at reduced resolution with upscaling (render to half-res FBO, upscale). And we need to handle the maze structure — an SDF from 3D noise gives organic caves but not guaranteed connectivity. We can use a maze-based SDF: distance to a set of corridors (union of capsules) → guaranteed connectivity! Carve tunnels along a generated maze graph, then subtract. That's excellent: SDF = (distance to tunnel skeleton network) - radius, plus noise for wobble. And it's computable both on GPU (for rendering) and CPU (for collision) if the skeleton is passed as data.

But passing a big skeleton to the shader is awkward. Instead: the SDF can be defined analytically as: cells on a grid where corridors exist along edges. Distance to the union of segments. With a uniform array of segments (say up to 400 segments = 4002vec4 = 800 vec4s, over uniform limits maybe; typical max uniform vectors 1024-4096). Hmm. Could use a texture storing segment list, but branching to loop over 400 segments per ray step is expensive.

Alternative: make the cave a tunnel along a path — a single winding tube (like an "endless cave dive") rather than an open maze. SDF = distance to a parametric curve - radius. The curve can be computed on GPU procedurally from hash noise (a smooth random walk) — but then CPU/GPU must agree exactly; we can implement the same deterministic value-noise in both JS and GLSL using integer hashing that is bit-exact... risky but possible if careful (avoid float precision issues; use same operations). Risky.

Better approach: hybrid. Precompute on CPU a path as an array of points (a spline through a maze). Send it to the shader via a texture (a 2D texture where each texel = vec4 point). Then for SDF, find nearest path segment by looping over a limited window of segments around the player's current index (we know approximately where the player is; but rays go far...). Limit raymarching distance (fog / max distance ~ 40 units) so only nearby segments matter: pass a sliding window of ~48 segments as a uniform array (48*2 vec4 = 96 vec4s) centered on the player. Rays march up to 60 units, and segments outside the window would be missed — but the tunnel is continuous and the window covers ±48 segments which at ~3 units per segment = 144 units. Fine! That's a great trick: sliding window of tunnel segments in uniforms.

So the world: a long winding tunnel network (a maze tree/graph flattened into segments). Actually if it's a tree with branches, a "sliding window" of a linear path doesn't cover branches. Unless we order segments by proximity to player each frame — that's a nice solution: each frame, pick the K nearest segments to the camera (K=64) and upload. Then rays only see those. Branch geometry beyond K nearest segments would be invisible — but those are far away anyway, and fog hides them. However, occlusion issues: a nearby segment might be excluded if there are 64 nearer ones... we sort by distance to camera and take 64 nearest; distant ones are fogged out. With a maze density where 64 segments covers a radius of ~20-30 units, and fog at 30 units, it's consistent. Risk: visible popping at the fog edge — acceptable, hidden by fog.

This is getting complex but it's genuinely doable and would look amazing. But complexity → risk of a broken build. The instructions warn: a broken ambitious build scores worse than a clean simple one.

Let me weigh: I have 25 tool calls. I can write a big single file in maybe 2-4 create/edit calls. Debugging without a browser to test... I can't run the code! That's the key risk: no execution feedback. I must write very careful, defensive WebGL2 code with fallbacks.

Given no ability to test, I should choose an architecture where correctness is more predictable. Let me consider: WebGL2 full-screen raymarcher with a small uniform array. The shader math is the risky part. Alternative: WebGL2 rasterized voxels with greedy meshing — also substantial code but more predictable (no SDF math bugs, no raymarch artifacts), and it can look great with baked AO + fog + emissive + post-processing bloom.

Actually, here's a middle ground that is very reliable AND looks stunning: raymarched SDF cave but with the SDF being simple: distance to a capsule-chain (list of segments) in a uniform array, plus noise displacement. I've written these before; the math is straightforward. The main risks:

Loop over 64 segments per ray step: expensive (64 * ~60 steps = 3840 evaluations). Too slow! Need optimization: precompute nearest-segment per step? Standard trick: compute d = min over segments at each step — that's the cost. 64 segments × 80 steps = 5120 min ops per pixel → at 1080p that's 10 GFLOP-ish... too slow.*
Optimization: Use a bounding volume hierarchy or just limit segments to ~24 and use larger step sizes with a "tube" SDF where we can bound. Alternatively: since the tunnel is essentially a tube around a path, we can render the complement: the player is INSIDE the tunnel. So we're raymarching inside a cave: we need to find where the ray hits the wall. Distance to wall = radius - distToPath (when inside the tube). d = min over segments of (radius - distToSeg)? That's the SDF of the complement of the union of capsules (approximately). Same cost.

Cheaper: reduce segments to 16-24 (nearest ones) and use fog distance 25 units. 24 × 60 steps = 1440 ops/pixel. At 960×540 = 518k pixels → 750M ops. Modern GPU: ~fine (that's like a few GFLOPs). Render at 0.6-0.75 resolution scale with a sharpen-upscale. Feasible.

Hmm, but honestly, for reliability, I lean toward a rasterized voxel/quad cave with high polish. But voxel cubes = "2013 minecraft look" unless I do something special. I could do greedy-meshed voxels with per-fragment triplanar noise + baked AO + normal reconstruction from noise for "eroded" look... still blocky.

Third option: marching cubes on CPU at load for a blobby cave → smooth organic mesh, baked AO via vertex ambient from a small sample, then rasterize with nice shaders (triplanar rock texture procedurally in fragment shader, fresnel, fog, emissive crystals) + bloom post-processing. Marching cubes on CPU: ~200 lines, well-understood, deterministic. Grid of 96×96×96 = 884k cells → too slow in JS? Marching cubes at 96^3 ≈ 884k cells, each cell needs 8 SDF samples. With a cached scalar grid (98^3 ≈ 941k floats) it's maybe 200-500ms in JS. Acceptable with a loading screen. Mesh could be ~500k triangles... heavy but fine (static VBO). Actually let's do 80×80×80 = 512k cells → maybe 150-300k triangles. That's fine for one draw call.

But collision in a marching-cubes world requires SDF sampling on CPU → we already have the scalar grid, so we can do a nice trilinear-sampled collision + sliding. Good.

Maze guarantee: build the cave as distance-to-maze-corridors minus radius, with noise. The maze (recursive-backtracker on a grid of cells, e.g., 16×16) gives guaranteed connectivity, and carve capsules. SDF = min over corridor capsules (dist - radius) + noise*wobble... plus we can clamp wobble so it never fully blocks (wobble amplitude < radius margin). Good: guaranteed passable.

The scalar field F(p) = min over all corridor segments of (|p - seg| - R) + fbm noise. Then mesh where F = 0 (inside = F<0 → solid? Let's define: solid where F < 0 → is that right? If dist to corridor < R then we're in the tunnel (open). So open where (distToCorridor - R) + noise > 0... Let's define D(p) = min over segments (distToSeg(p) - R). Open where D > 0. Add noise: Open where D + n(p)a > 0 with a small. Actually to guarantee passage, use open where D + noise > -0.15... hmm. Simply: D_open(p) = D(p) + noise(p) where noise has amplitude 0.6 and R = 3.0. Since |D| at corridor center is 3, the noise can't seal it (needs noise < -3). Safe. Great.

Emissive crystals: place at random positions inside walls near tunnels.

Now, the "reveal" mechanic: each vertex needs to know when it was last illuminated by a ping wavefront. In the fragment shader we can compute this analytically! We know ping origin(s) and times (keep last ~6 pings as uniforms: vec4(origin.xyz, time)). For a fragment at position p, for each ping: revealAmount = f(distance(p, origin) - speed*(now - t)). That's a wavefront expanding from the origin — cheap and beautiful: the wall lights up in a spherical shell expanding outward, and then fades over time. This works with rasterized geometry in the fragment shader.*

So the visual: pitch black. Ping → expanding sphere of light sweeps across the cave walls, revealing geometry briefly in cyan/teal, with the fresnel edge glowing, then decaying to black over ~2.5s. Additional "bioluminescent" accents (crystals) that are always faintly visible — giving the player minimal orientation. Plus a subtle "aurora" of floating dust particles that catch the ping.

This is genuinely beautiful and thematically coherent. And the interaction thesis is strong: you must act to see. Echo = risk (attracts the Hunter, costs breath/energy).

Game systems:

Oxygen/Breath: drains constantly (e.g., 100 → 0 over ~3.5 min). Fail state: 0 = blackout, game over.

Alternative: Surface Nets (Dual Contouring-lite): For each cell that contains a sign change, place a vertex at the average of edge crossings, then for each of the 3 axes connecting to neighbor cells, emit a quad connecting the 4 cell-vertices. This is much easier to implement correctly from scratch (no lookup tables!) and produces smooth, watertight-ish meshes. Yes! Naive Surface Nets is a great choice: ~80 lines, no magic tables, smooth blobs. I've seen it implemented compactly (Mikola Lysenko's isometrify). Let me write it carefully myself.

Surface nets algorithm:

Grid of dimensions (nx, ny, nz) of scalar values. Define f(i,j,k).
For each cell (i,j,k) with i<nx-1 etc.: compute the 8 corner values; if all same sign → no vertex. Else compute vertex position = average of edge intersection points (edges where sign changes, linearly interpolated), in world coords. Store vertex index in cellIndex[i,j,k].
Then for each cell and each of 3 axes (x, y, z), if neighbor cell in that direction also has a vertex, and the 4 cells forming the shared face straddle signs, emit a quad. Standard condition: for edge along x between cell (i,j,k) and (i+1,j,k): check the 2×2 block of cells (i, j..j+1, k..k+1) — if all 4 have vertices and the "edge" corners have differing signs, emit quad. Simplified: emit quad if all 4 cells have vertices and the sign of the corner values differ... Let me recall Mikola's implementation:


for each cell (i,j,k) in [1..n-2]? 
  ...
  // edges
  for d in 0..2:
    ...
Mikola's surfacenets code:

js


for(var i=0; i<n[0]-1; ++i)
for(var j=0; j<n[1]-1; ++j)
for(var k=0; k<n[2]-1; ++k) {
  var i0 = buffer_index(i,j,k), i1 = buffer_index(i+1,j,k) ...
  // corners: c = [f(i,j,k), f(i+1,j,k), f(i,j+1,k), ...]
  var mask = 0, g = 0, bits = [1,2,4,8,16,32,64,128];
  for(var l=0; l<8; ++l) { if(corner[l] < 0) { mask |= bits[l]; g += 1 } }
  if (mask === 0 || mask === 255) continue;   // no crossing
  // vertex = average of edge crossings
  ...
  vertex_index[i0] = vertices.length/3
  // then quad emission:
  if (i > 0 && j > 0) ... 
}
Actually the emission in his code:

js


//Add faces together, if appropriate
for(var k=0; k<3; ++k) {
  //The first three entries of the edge_mask count the crossings along the edge
  if(!(edge_mask & (1<<k)) ) continue;
  // i = axes we're point along.  iu, iv = orthogonal axes
  var iu = (k+1)%3, iv = (k+2)%3;
  //If we are on a boundary, skip it
  if(x[iu] === 0 || x[iv] === 0) continue;
  //Otherwise, look up adjacent edges in buffer
  var du = R[iu], dv = R[iv];
  //Remember to flip orientation depending on the sign of the corner.
  if(mask & 1) {
    faces.push([v, cellIndex[iu], cellIndex[iu+dv], cellIndex[iu+du+dv], ...]);
  } else { reverse }
}
where edge_mask bit k is set if the sign changes along edge k from corner 0 to corner 2^k (i.e., f(p) and f(p + e_k) differ).

Let me just re-derive from scratch, careful and simple:

Consider cell C at grid position (i,j,k) with corner positions. Take the edge from corner c0 = (i,j,k) to corner c_{2^k} = (i + ek). If F(c0) and F(c_{2^k}) have different signs, then the surface crosses this edge. The surface quad associated with this edge connects the surface-net vertices of the 4 cells that share this edge: cells (i,j,k), (i - eu, j, k), (i - eu - ev, j, k), (i - ev, j, k) where eu, ev are the two axes other than k. Wait — which 4 cells share the edge from c0 in direction ek? The edge is at grid position (i,j,k) going in direction ek. Cells sharing it are those with lower corner at (i - a, j - b, k) for a,b ∈ {0,1} in the two orthogonal axes u,v. Yes: cells C00 = (i,j,k), C10 = (i-eu), C11 = (i-eu-ev), C01 = (i-ev).

So: quad vertices in order: V(i,j,k), V(i-ev), V(i-eu-ev), V(i-eu) — going around. Winding: need consistent orientation so normals point out of solid (toward open space). If F(c0) > 0 (open at c0) and F(c_{2^k}) < 0 (solid), then... Let's just compute the normal from the quad and flip if it points toward the solid side. Easier: after generating, compute per-quad normal via cross product, and compare with the gradient of F at the quad center (which points toward increasing F = open). If dot < 0, flip winding. That's robust and easy: I have F on the grid._

Also compute per-vertex normals via gradient of F (trilinear/central differences) — smooth normals. And per-vertex AO: estimate by sampling F along the normal direction: ao = clamp(1 - k*(number of samples that are solid)) — or simpler: sample F at p + ns for s in [0.15, 0.4, 0.8] and if solid → occlusion. Cheap and gives nice contact darkening. Actually since it's a cave, ambient occlusion from concavity: sample along the normal (into open space) and check for nearby solid → occluded. Good.

Alternatively compute AO in the fragment shader with a few SDF samples — no, vertex AO is fine and cheap.

SDF: for point p, d = min over all corridor segments of distToSegment(p) - R where R = 2.6. Number of segments for 12×12 maze: ~143 edges. Distance to 143 segments per grid sample × 269k samples = 38M segment-distance computations. That's too slow in JS (~1-3 seconds). Optimize: for each grid sample, only check segments whose XZ cell is within some radius — build a spatial hash: for each maze cell, list of segments touching it and neighbors. Since the grid is aligned with maze cells (cell size 6, grid 1), for each grid sample, find maze cell, check segments in a 5×5 neighborhood (25 segments max). 269k × 25 = 6.7M — fine (~100ms).

Cave mesh shader (vertex)
glsl


#version 300 es
precision highp float;
in vec3 aPos;      // world position
in vec3 aNormal;
in float aAO;
in float aMat;     // material id / crystal vein factor
uniform mat4 uViewProj;
uniform vec3 uCam;
out vec3 vWorld;
out vec3 vNormal;
out float vAO;
out float vMat;
void main(){
  vWorld = aPos; vNormal = aNormal; vAO = aAO; vMat = aMat;
  gl_Position = uViewProj * vec4(aPos, 1.0);
}
Fragment:

glsl


#version 300 es
precision highp float;
in vec3 vWorld; in vec3 vNormal; in float vAO; in float vMat;
uniform vec3 uCam; uniform float uTime;
uniform vec4 uPings[8];   // xyz origin, w start time (or -1)
uniform int uPingCount;
uniform vec3 uLights[10]; // xyz pos
uniform vec4 uLightCol[10]; // rgb + intensity
uniform int uLightCount;
uniform float uPlayerGlow;
out vec4 fragColor;
// hash noise for rock texture
...
void main(){
  vec3 N = normalize(vNormal);
  vec3 V = normalize(uCam - vWorld);
  // triplanar rock noise
  float n = fbm(vWorld*0.6);
  float veins = ... 
  vec3 base = mix(vec3(0.06,0.09,0.11), vec3(0.10,0.14,0.16), n);
  ...
  // player light
  // crystal lights
  // echo reveal
  float reveal = 0.0; vec3 echoCol = vec3(0);
  for(int i=0;i<8;i++){
     if(i>=uPingCount) break;
     vec4 P = uPings[i];
     float dt = uTime - P.w; if(dt<0.0) continue;
     float r = length(vWorld - P.xyz);
     float front = dt * 14.0;
     // wavefront band
     float band = exp(-pow((r-front)/2.2, 2.0)) * 1.2;
     // lingering afterglow
     float lag = dt - r/14.0;
     float after = lag > 0.0 ? exp(-lag*0.85) : 0.0;
     float amt = band*0.9 + after*0.85;
     vec3 c = mix(vec3(0.55,1.0,1.0), vec3(0.15,0.45,0.8), clamp(r/26.0,0.,1.));
     echoCol += c * amt;
  }
  ...
}
The wavefront should also be directional — the echo reveals surfaces facing the origin more: multiply by max(dot(N, dir), 0.15) where dir = normalize(vWorld - origin) — actually the surface faces the origin, so dot(N, normalize(origin - vWorld)) > 0. Use that for shading (like a point light). Great: this gives real point-light shading from the ping, which looks like a flash of light.

So: echo = pointLight(origin, color * intensity(t)) where intensity is a pulse (band) — plus afterglow that decays. This is elegant: treat ping as a moving/shrinking point light? No — an expanding shell that lights everything it passes. The "band" approach lights a shell; but I also want the whole surface to remain lit briefly. My "after" term handles that (after the shell passes, the surface stays lit and decays). Combined: shell gives a bright leading edge (with distance falloff), after gives lingering.*

Let me make ping falloff: intensity ∝ 1/(1+rr0.01) roughly, times shell/after terms. Also cap by max echo radius (~30 for big ping, ~9 for whisper). Encode whisper as smaller radius: use ping.w = time, and an extra array for radius? Pack: uPings[i] = (origin.xyz, time) and uPingR[i] = (radius, intensity, decay, 0). Two arrays of 8. Fine.

Post shader


vec3 c = texture(uScene, uv).rgb;
vec3 b = texture(uBloom, uv).rgb;
// chromatic aberration on scene
c.r = texture(uScene, uv + ca).r; c.b = texture(uScene, uv - ca).b;
vec3 col = c + b*1.1;
col = tonemap_ACES(col * exposure);
col *= vignette;
col += grain;
Plus a red danger pulse (uniform uDanger) and a "damage" flash that decays.

Game loop / states
states: 'title', 'playing', 'paused', 'dead', 'won'. On death: show cause, stats, restart (new seed or same seed).

Progression
Dives (levels): each level = new maze with more shards required and a faster Hunter. Score = depth. On completing a dive, "descend" to the next (regenerate). Endless with increasing difficulty, so there's always resistance. Win screen after each dive with "Descend deeper" button.

Oxygen: 100 units, drain 1.35/s (~74s) — too short. Let's set drain 0.55/s (~180s = 3 min). Shards give +18 oxygen each. Need 5 shards to open the exit. That's a real time pressure. Hunter adds threat.

Echo energy: 100; big ping costs 25, regen 9/s. Whisper costs 6, regen same. So you can't spam big pings (max ~4 in a row). Movement sprint costs oxygen faster.

Hunter AI:

State: 'idle' (drifting slowly along the maze), 'hunting' (moves toward last heard ping origin), 'searching' (wanders near last known), 'chasing' (if it can see/sense you within 6 units and you recently pinged or moved fast).
Movement along maze corridors: to move to a target, do BFS on the maze graph from its cell to the target cell, follow the path. That's clean and makes it feel like it knows the cave.
Speed: 2.6 units/s hunting, 1.1 idle. Player speed: 3.4 walk, 5.2 sprint. So the player can outrun it, but sprint costs oxygen and makes noise (which attracts it).

Audio synthesis (WebAudio)
master gain → compressor → destination.
Ping: oscillator sine sweep 900→180 Hz over 0.5s with a bandpass, plus a short noise burst; a convolver-less "reverb" via feedback delay (2 delays with feedback 0.35, times 0.13/0.19) → cave reverb. Nice.
Whisper: shorter, quieter, higher.
Shard pickup: bell (2 sines at 5ths, fast decay) + reverb.
Beacon: pulsing pad.
Hunter: sawtooth low (55 Hz) with LFO + noise, gain tied to proximity.
Ambience: very low filtered brown noise, gain 0.06 (subtle) + slow sine drone at 40 Hz. Careful about the "no constant drone" rule for mute — mute kills master gain instantly, fine.
Death: descending noise whoosh.
Footstep/movement: soft filtered noise whoosh when sprinting.

Let me now think about the collision + movement details:



let pos = vec3, vel = vec3.
input accel in camera-yaw space (horizontal) + world up/down.
vel += accel*dt; vel *= damping (e.g., exp(-3.2*dt)) — swim feel.
pos += vel*dt.
// collision: 3 iterations
for (i<4) { const f = sampleF(pos); if (f < 0.42) { const g = gradF(pos); const n = normalize(g); pos += n * (0.42 - f); vel -= n*dot(vel,n) * 1.0 } }
Note sign convention: F > 0 open, F < 0 solid. Gradient of F points toward increasing openness. Pushing out along +gradient. Good.

sampleF uses trilinear interpolation over the scalar grid — must match the mesh (mesh is from the same grid, so it's consistent up to interpolation). Good.

Also clamp pos to grid bounds.

World bounds: the outer boundary — the maze is surrounded by rock (cells outside the maze are solid). Since the SDF is min over corridor segments, far from corridors F is large positive?? WAIT. Bug: F = (distToNearestCorridor - R) + noise. If F > 0 means open, then everywhere far from corridors is open — the whole world is open space! I have the sign backwards. I need: solid where far from corridors. So define F such that F < 0 = solid: F = R - minDistToCorridor + noise... then near corridor center F = R = 2.6 > 0 (open); far away F = -large (solid). Yes! F = R - d + noise, where d = min distance to corridor skeleton. Open where F > 0. That works: far from any corridor, d is huge → F very negative → solid.

So marching: surface at F = 0. Solid = F < 0. Gradient points from solid to open... gradient of (R - d) = -∇d, and ∇d points away from the corridor (increasing distance). So -∇d points toward the corridor = toward open. Correct: gradient points toward open space. Push out along +grad.

Now for the noise: add noise to make the walls organic. F = R - d + n where n ∈ [-1.2, 1.2]. Also add a "floor bias"? Not needed.

Also we want the cave to have varied cross-section: use R varying per segment (chambers bigger). d = min over segments of (distToSeg - segRadius)? Then F = -d' + n where d' = min(distToSeg - R_seg). Hmm: define d' = min over segments (distToSeg(p) - R_seg). Then near the corridor center d' = -R (negative). Open where -d' + n > 0 → d' < n. Ugh, let me just define:

SDF_open(p) = min over segments (R_seg - distToSeg(p)) + noise. This is the "signed distance to the corridor union, positive inside". Then F = SDF_open + noise, open where F > 0, solid where F < 0. Far away, SDF_open is very negative → solid.

Now, noise amplitude must be small enough not to break the corridor: at center SDF_open = R_seg (2.6), so with |noise| ≤ 1.2 it stays open. But near the corridor edge the noise creates bumps — that's the organic look.

One issue: infinite world? No — grid is finite: 96×96 XZ over a 12×12 maze of 6-unit cells = 72 units + margins. Let's set maze 13×13 cells × 6 units = 78 units. But resolution 1.2 units per grid cell for surface nets — the mesh will be coarse (corridor radius 2.6 → ~4 cells across). function frame(t){

  updateHud();
}

Let me write out the shaders now in full detail.

CAVE VERTEX SHADER

glsl


#version 300 es
precision highp float;
layout(location=0) in vec3 aPos;
layout(location=1) in vec3 aNrm;
layout(location=2) in float aAO;
uniform mat4 uVP;
uniform vec3 uCam;
out vec3 vW; out vec3 vN; out float vAO;
void main(){
  vW = aPos; vN = aNrm; vAO = aAO;
  gl_Position = uVP * vec4(aPos,1.0);
}
CAVE FRAGMENT SHADER

glsl


#version 300 es
precision highp float;
in vec3 vW; in vec3 vN; in float vAO;
uniform vec3 uCam;
uniform float uTime;
uniform vec4 uPing[8];     // xyz = origin, w = start time
uniform vec4 uPingP[8];    // x = radius, y = intensity, z = decay, w = unused
uniform int uPingN;
uniform vec4 uLight[12];   // xyz pos, w = radius
uniform vec4 uLightC[12];  // rgb color, a intensity
uniform int uLightN;
uniform float uGlow;       // player glow intensity
uniform float uHue;
out vec4 outColor;
float hash(vec3 p){ p = fract(p*0.3183099 + vec3(0.1,0.2,0.3)); p *= 17.0; return fract(p.x*p.y*p.z*(p.x+p.y+p.z)); }
float vnoise(vec3 x){
  vec3 i = floor(x), f = fract(x);
  f = f*f*(3.0-2.0*f);
  float n000=hash(i+vec3(0,0,0)), n100=hash(i+vec3(1,0,0));
  ...
  return mix(mix(mix(n000,n100,f.x), mix(n010,n110,f.x), f.y), mix(mix(n001,n101,f.x), mix(n011,n111,f.x), f.y), f.z);
}
float fbm(vec3 p){ float a=0.5, s=0.0; for(int i=0;i<4;i++){ s += a*vnoise(p); p*=2.02; a*=0.5;} return s; }
Careful: hash with fract(p*0.3183+...) — standard iq hash. OK.

Rock color + veins + lighting:

glsl


void main(){
  vec3 N = normalize(vN);
  // bump
  float e = 0.12;
  float h0 = fbm(vW*1.7);
  float hx = fbm((vW+vec3(e,0,0))*1.7);
  float hy = fbm((vW+vec3(0,e,0))*1.7);
  float hz = fbm((vW+vec3(0,0,e))*1.7);
  N = normalize(N - (vec3(hx-h0, hy-h0, hz-h0)/e) * 0.06);
  vec3 V = normalize(uCam - vW);
  ...
}
That's 4 fbm calls (16 noise calls) + veins fbm. Might be heavy but OK at reduced res. Let me reduce: use 3-octave fbm for the bump and compute the perturbation with 3 extra samples (total 4 fbm of 3 octaves = 12 noise evals). Acceptable.

Lighting accumulation:

glsl


  vec3 col = vec3(0.0);
  // rock base albedo
  float rn = fbm(vW*0.8);
  vec3 albedo = mix(vec3(0.055,0.075,0.095), vec3(0.11,0.15,0.17), rn);
  // glowing veins
  float vein = smoothstep(0.62, 0.72, fbm(vW*0.45 + 11.0));
  albedo += vec3(0.05,0.35,0.42) * vein * 1.4;
  float emis = vein * 0.5;
  
  // player glow
  {
    vec3 L = uCam - vW; float d = length(L); L/=d;
    float att = uGlow / (1.0 + d*d*0.55);
    col += albedo * att * max(dot(N,L),0.0) * vec3(0.35,0.75,0.85);
  }
  // lights
  for(int i=0;i<12;i++){ if(i>=uLightN) break; ... }
  // echo
  for(int i=0;i<8;i++){ if(i>=uPingN) break; ... }
Note: break in a loop with a uniform int condition is allowed in GLSL ES 3.0 (dynamic loops are fine in ES 3.0). Yes, WebGL2/GLSL ES 3.00 supports dynamic loops.

Echo term:

glsl


  for(int i=0;i<8;i++){
    if(i>=uPingN) break;
    vec4 P = uPing[i]; vec4 Q = uPingP[i];
    float dt = uTime - P.w;
    if(dt <= 0.0) continue;
    vec3 dv = vW - P.xyz; float r = length(dv);
    if(r > Q.x) continue;
    vec3 L = -dv/max(r,0.001);
    float sp = 16.0;
    float front = dt*sp;
    float lag = dt - r/sp;
    float band = exp(-pow((r-front)*0.55, 2.0));
    float after = lag > 0.0 ? exp(-lag*Q.z) : 0.0;
    float fade = 1.0 - smoothstep(Q.x*0.75, Q.x, r);
    float ndl = max(dot(N,L), 0.08) + 0.25*pow(1.0-abs(dot(N,L)), 3.0); // wrap
    float inten = Q.y * (band*1.6 + after) * fade / (1.0 + r*r*0.008);
    vec3 c = mix(vec3(0.75,1.0,1.0), vec3(0.18,0.5,0.85), clamp(r/Q.x,0.0,1.0));
    col += albedo * c * inten * ndl;
    col += c * inten * 0.12 * vAO; // slight fog glow
  }
Hmm the ping color: also allow per-ping color (whisper = violet, beacon = gold). Add color to uPingP as... I need xyz? Let me restructure: uPingP[i] = (radius, intensity, decay, hue) where hue selects a color ramp: 0 = cyan, 1 = gold, 0.5 = violet. I'll do vec3 c = pingColor(Q.w) with a mix chain. Good.

Fog: add depth-based fog toward black (well, everything is black anyway) — but with echo-lit fog: skip. Actually the echo "fog glow" gives a volumetric feel.

Ambient: col += albedo * vec3(0.012,0.02,0.03) * vAO; — barely visible.

Final: outColor = vec4(col * vAO, 1.0); where vAO bakes in occlusion.*

AO computed at mesh time: sample F at p + N*s. Let me implement:

js

Post shader:

glsl


uniform sampler2D uScene, uBloom;
uniform vec2 uRes; uniform float uTime, uDanger, uFlash, uExposure, uPulse;
in vec2 vUv; out vec4 o;
void main(){
  vec2 uv = vUv;
  vec2 d = uv - 0.5;
  float r2 = dot(d,d);
  vec2 ca = d * (0.0015 + uDanger*0.004 + r2*0.004);
  vec3 c;
  c.r = texture(uScene, uv + ca).r;
  c.g = texture(uScene, uv).g;
  c.b = texture(uScene, uv - ca).b;
  vec3 b = texture(uBloom, uv).rgb;
  c += b * (1.15 + uPulse*0.4);
  c *= uExposure;
  // tonemap
  c = (c*(2.51*c+0.03))/(c*(2.43*c+0.59)+0.14);
  // vignette
  c *= smoothstep(0.95, 0.25, r2*1.6) * 0.85 + 0.15;
  // danger tint at edges
  c += vec3(0.55,0.05,0.06) * uDanger * smoothstep(0.06, 0.42, r2) * (0.6+0.4*sin(uTime*7.0));
  // grain
  float g = fract(sin(dot(uv*uRes + uTime*60.0, vec2(12.9898,78.233)))*43758.5453);
  c += (g-0.5)*0.035;
  // flash (whiteout that decays)
  c += uFlash * vec3(0.6,0.9,1.0);
  o = vec4(max(c, 0.0), 1.0);
}
Good. uFlash decays in JS.
